Balanced bipartite distance of K 4 K_{4} -free graphs
József Balogh, Ignacy Buczek, Andrzej Grzesik, Piotr Kuc

Abstract
We show that every K 4 K_{4} -free graph on n n vertices can be made balanced bipartite by removing at most n 2 9 \frac{n^{2}}{9} edges. This proves a conjecture of Balogh, Clemen, and Lidický, and generalizes both Sudakov’s result on the bipartite distance of K 4 K_{4} -free graphs and Reiher’s result on the sparse half of K 4 K_{4} -free graphs.

†

1
Introduction

The famous
Sparse half conjecture
of Erdős
[
12
]
, open for over 50 years, states that every
n
-vertex triangle-free graph has a subset of vertices of size
\frac{n}{2}
that spans at most
\frac{n^{2}}{50}
edges.
This conjecture received a lot of attention, see
[
9
,
19
,
22
,
28
]
, with Razborov achieving the strongest known general bound of
\frac{27n^{2}}{1024}
in
[
28
]
.

A similar problem, the
Sparse halves conjecture
, which asks for the minimum number of edges that must be removed from a triangle-free graph to make it bipartite, was posed by Erdős
[
11
]
even earlier.
This question is closely related to the
Sparse half conjecture
, as it is expected that the tight lower bounds for both problems are attained by the same extremal graph (a balanced blowup of a pentagon).
Because of this, it is believed that to make a triangle-free graph bipartite, it is always sufficient to remove
\frac{n^{2}}{50}\cdot 2=\frac{n^{2}}{25}
edges.
This problem is also difficult and has attracted considerable attention
[
2
,
8
,
10
]
.
The best known general bound is
\frac{2n^{2}}{47}
, which was recently proved in
[
2
]
.

It is important to note that there is no implication relationship between the
Sparse half conjecture
and the
Sparse halves conjecture
, which is why they gave rise to two relatively independent research directions.
A pertinent question is that if these problems have an intuitive generalization, with a natural candidate being a hypothetical bound on the number of edges that have to be removed to make a triangle-free graph balanced bipartite.
If the tight bound was identical to the
Sparse halves conjecture
and equaled
\frac{n^{2}}{25}
, it would imply both of the conjectures.
This question was initially asked and answered by Balogh, Clemen, and Lidický in
[
3
]
, with the answer being unfortunately negative.
It is easy to see that to make the complete bipartite graph with parts of sizes
\frac{3n}{4}
and
\frac{n}{4}
balanced bipartite, one always has to remove at least
\frac{n^{2}}{16}
edges.
Balogh, Clemen, and Lidický (
[
3
]
) proved that this is the optimal bound in general.
This implies that, surprisingly, proving a tight bound for this problem is significantly easier than for the unbalanced one.

Theorem 1.1

(Balogh, Clemen, and Lidický
[
3
]
)
.

Every triangle-free graph on
n
vertices can be made balanced bipartite by removing at most
\frac{n^{2}}{16}
edges.

The motive of asking how far from being bipartite a graph can be is a recurring theme in Erdős’ work.
Not long after asking this question for the class of triangle-free graphs, he
[
8
]
formulated a similar problem in the
K_{4}
-free setting, where
he conjectured that the complete tripartite graph is the extremal example.
This was proved by Sudakov
[
31
]
.

Theorem 1.2

(Sudakov
[
31
]
)
.

Every
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices can be made bipartite by removing at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}
edges.

The
Sparse half conjecture
also has a natural variant for
K_{4}
-free graphs, see
[
8
]
, which was posed by Chung and Graham
[
7
]
and independently by Erdős, Faudree, Rousseau and Schelp
[
9
]
.
Both groups anticipated that the tight bound should be once again attained by the complete balanced tripartite graph.
Confirming this bound seemed more elusive than proving the tight bound on the bipartite distance, but building on the partial progress of Liu and Ma (
[
21
]
), who settled the conjecture for regular graphs, Reiher
[
29
]
recently managed to do so.

Theorem 1.3

(Reiher
[
29
]
)
.

Every
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices has a subset of
\frac{n}{2}
vertices which spans at most
\frac{n^{2}}{18}
edges.

Analogously to the
Sparse half conjecture
and the
Sparse halves conjecture
,

Theorem 1.2
and
Theorem 1.3
could be potentially generalized by a sufficiently strong bound on the balanced bipartite distance in the class of
K_{4}
-free graphs.

Theorem 1.1
naturally suggests that such a bound is unlikely to be obtained.
Intriguingly, Balogh, Clemen, and Lidický
[
3
]
noticed that the usual interesting candidates in the
K_{4}
-free class are not sufficient to exclude the possibility of such a bound, which led them to conjecture that it might actually be achievable.

1.4
Balogh, Clemen, and Lidický
[
3
]
.

Every
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices can be made balanced bipartite by removing at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}
edges.

In this work, we show that
1.4
holds.
This directly generalizes, and gives a different proof of the aforementioned results of Sudakov (
[
31
]
) and Reiher (
[
29
]
).
Moreover, it confirms an unexpected discrepancy between the classes of triangle-free and
K_{4}
-free graphs.

The proof consists of two major steps.
First, we show
1.4
for all graphs that are sufficiently close to the complete balanced tripartite graph.
In the second step, we introduce an improved approach of describing graphs cuts in Razborov’s flag algebra framework (
[
24
]
) to show that a potential counterexample would need to be very close to the complete balanced tripartite graph, which is not possible due to the previously proven statement.

It is worth noting that while the idea of combining flag algebras with graph cuts has been used in the literature (
[
3
]
,
[
2
]
), its usual application is not suitable for this problem or most partitioning problems that impose requirements on the sizes of the components in the partition.
We overcome this obstacle by applying flag algebras to a vertex-colored graph derived from the potential counterexample, where colors of vertices encode extra information that gives more control of the partitions.
We believe that this approach should be applicable to other partitioning problems and should lead to further progress on the
Sparse half conjecture
and
Sparse half conjectures
.

The paper is organized as follows.
In
Section 2
we introduce basic graph notation used in the paper.

Section 3
is devoted to proving some basic subcases of
1.4
that will be needed later.
In
Section 4
we include the proof of
1.4
for graphs that contain a large pair of independent sets.
This result is used in
Section 5
that solves the conjecture in the close neighborhood of the extremal example.
To finish the proof in the general case, we use the flag algebra framework, which we briefly introduce in
Section 6
.
The proof of our main result is completed in
Section 7
.

2
Notation

For a graph
G
and a vertex
v\in V(G)
, we define the
open neighborhood
of
v
in
G
as

N(v):=\{u\in V(G):(u,v)\in E(G)\}.

We define the
degree
of
v
in
G
as
\deg(v):=|N(v)|
and the
minimum degree
of
G
as
\delta(G):=\min_{v\in V(G)}\deg(v).

Similarly, for a pair of vertices
u,v\in V(G)
, we define the
open common neighborhood
of
u
and
v
in
G
as

N(u,v):=\{w\in V(G):(u,w),(v,w)\in E(G)\},

and the
common degree
of
u,v
in
G
as
\deg(u,v):=|N(u,v)|
.

For a graph
G
and a set of vertices
U\subseteq V(G)
, we define the
open neighborhood
of
U
in
G
as

N(U):=\bigcup\{N(v):v\in U\}\setminus U.

We define
G[U]
as the subgraph of
G
induced on
U
, that is, a graph with vertex set
U
and edge set
\{(u,v)\in E(G):u,v\in U\}
.
If
U
is equal to
V(G)\setminus\{v\}
for some
v\in V(G)
, we simply write
G-v
.
For simplicity, we denote
E(U):=E(G[U])
and
e(U):=|E(U)|
. Whenever the graph
G
is not clear from the context, we add subscript
G
and write
e_{G}(U)
.

Analogously, for a graph
G
and sets of vertices
U,W\subseteq V(G)
, we denote the set of edges of
G
that are between
U
and
W
as

E(U,W):=\{(u,v)\in E(G):u\in U,v\in W\}

and their number by

e(U,W):=|E(U,W)|.

If
U=\{v\}
for some
v\in V(G)
, we use a short-hand syntax
E(v,W)
, and
e(v,W)
.

A
k
-vertex-colored graph
is a pair
(G,\theta)
, where
G
is a graph and
\theta
is a mapping from
V(G)
to
[k]:=\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
If the mapping
\theta
is clear from the context, we refer to the
k
-vertex-colored graph
(G,\theta)
as
G
.

Definition 2.1
.

For a graph
G
we define a
k
-blowup
of
G
as the following graph
H
:

•

V(H):=\bigcup\{\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}:v\in V(G)\}
,

•

E(H):=\bigcup\{\{(u_{i},v_{j}):1\leq i,j\leq k\}:(u,v)\in E(G)\}
.

For an
\ell
-vertex-colored graph
(G,\theta)
, we define its
k
-blowup as an
\ell
-vertex-colored graph
(H,\theta^{\prime})
, where
H
is the
k
-blowup of
G
and the mapping
\theta^{\prime}
is defined as

\theta^{\prime}(v_{i})=\theta(v)
for every
1\leq i\leq k
and every
v\in V(G)
.

Definition 2.2
.

For a graph
G
, we define the
bipartite distance
of
G
as

\texttt{b}(G):=\min_{A\subseteq V(G)}e(A)+e(V(G)\setminus A).

Definition 2.3
.

For a graph
G
, we define the
balanced bipartite distance
of
G
as

\texttt{bb}(G):=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subseteq V(G)\\
|A|=\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor\end{subarray}}e(A)+e(V(G)\setminus A).

Intuitively,
\texttt{b}(G)
is the number of edges one has to remove to make
G
bipartite, while
\texttt{bb}(G)
is the number of edges one has to remove to make
G
balanced bipartite.

Definition 2.4
.

For a graph
G
and a partition
(A,B)
of
V(G)
, we say that an edge
{(u,v)}
of
G
is a
class-edge
in the partition
(A,B)
, if
\{u,v\}\subseteq A
or
\{u,v\}\subseteq B
.

3
Simple cases

3.1
Divisibility by six

When arguing about balanced bipartitions, divisibility of the number of vertices plays a key role.
In most of our arguments, we frequently divide the number of vertices by
2
or
3
.
For that reason, we devote the first couple of arguments to showing that we only need to consider graphs, whose number of vertices is a multiply of
6
.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
1.4
holds for a
2
-blowup of a graph
G
, then it also holds for
G
.

Proof.

Let
n
be the order of
G
, and let
H
be the 2-blowup of
G
.
From the assumption, there exists a balanced bipartition
(A,B)
of
H
with at most
4n^{2}/9

class-edges.
W.l.o.g. we may assume that for any
v\in V(G)
, whose copies belong to different parts of the partition
(A,B)
, it holds
v_{1}\in A
and
v_{2}\in B
.

Now, assume there exist two different vertices
u,v\in V(G)
, whose copies are in different parts of the partition.
Let
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be the partition obtained by swapping
u_{2}
with
v_{1}
in
(A,B)
.
Notice that
e(u_{1},A^{\prime})=e(u_{2},A^{\prime})
and
e(u_{1},A^{\prime})\leq e(u_{1},A)
, because
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are surely disconnected and
u_{1}
might have been connected to
v_{1}
.
Using this observation, we can form an upper bound on the number of class-edges adjacent to any copy of
u
or
v
in the partition
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})

e(u_{1},A^{\prime})+e(u_{2},A^{\prime})+e(v_{1},B)+e(v_{2},B)\leq 2e(u_{1},A)+2e(v_{2},B).

Similarly, we can consider an analogous partition obtained by swapping
u_{1}
with
v_{2}
, and justify that all copies of
u
and
v
would have at most
2e(v_{1},A)+2e(u_{2},B)
adjacent class-edges.
In the partition
(A,B)
, the number of class-edges adjacent to every copy of
u
or
v
is exactly

e(u_{1},A)+e(v_{1},A)+e(u_{2},B)+e(v_{2},B),

so at least one of the swaps does not increase the number of class-edges.
Hence, we may assume that all but at most one vertex of
G
have both of their copies on one side of the partition
(A,B)
.

Define
A_{G}
as the set of vertices of
G
that have both of their copies in
A
, and analogously define
B_{G}
.
If
(A_{G},B_{G})
is a partition of
V(G)
, then it is easy to see that it has at most

e(A_{G})+e(B_{G})=\frac{1}{4}(e(A)+e(B))=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{4n^{2}}{9}=\frac{n^{2}}{9}

class-edges.
Now, assume there exists a vertex
v
in
V(G)\setminus(A_{G}\cup B_{G})
(we justified there can be at most one such vertex).
Notice that

e(A_{G})+e(B_{G})+\frac{1}{2}e(v,A_{G})+\frac{1}{2}e(v,B_{G})=\frac{1}{4}(e(A)+e(B)).

As a result, if we assign
v
randomly to
A_{G}
or
B_{G}
, we can obtain a balanced bipartition with at most
\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{4n^{2}}{9}=\frac{n^{2}}{9}
class-edges.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

If
1.4
holds for a
3
-blowup of a graph
G
, then it also holds for
G
.

Proof.

Let
n
be the order of
G
and let
H
be a
3
-blowup of
G
.
From the assumption, there exists a balanced bipartition
(A,B)
of
H
with at most
n^{2}
class-edges.
This time,
|A|
and
|B|
have to be divisible by
3
, which implies that either zero or at least two vertices of
G
can have their copies on different sides.
Let
u
and
v
be two vertices of
G
that have their copies on two different sides of the partition
(A,B)
.

Case 1
.

Both vertices
u
and
v
have the same number of copies in
A
.

W.l.o.g., we can assume that they have exactly one copy in
A
, and those copies are
u_{1}
and
v_{1}
.
Consider swaps
(u_{1},v_{2})
and
(u_{2},v_{1})
as in the proof of
3.1
.
At least one of these swaps does not increase the number of class-edges and both of these swaps increase the number of vertices that have all of their copies on one side of the partition.
Hence, in this case, we can make a swap to decrease the number of vertices of
G
with their copies on different sides of the partition
(A,B)
without increasing the number of class-edges.

Case 2
.

Vertices
u
and
v
have a different number of copies in
A
.

Assume, w.l.o.g., that
u_{1}
is the only copy of
u
in
A
and
v_{2}
,
v_{3}
are the copies of
v
in
A
.
Consider the natural swap of
u_{1}
with
v_{1}
and the less intuitive swap of
(u_{2},u_{3})
with
(v_{2},v_{3})
.
Analogously to the 2-blowup case, there are at most
3e(v_{2},A)+3e(u_{2},B)
class-edges adjacent to any copy of
u
or
v
in the partition created by the first swap,
at most
3e(u_{1},A)+3e(v_{1},B)
in the partition created by the second swap, and exactly
2e(v_{2},A)+e(u_{1},A)+2e(u_{2},B)+e(v_{1},B)
in the partition
(A,B)
.
It holds

2(3e(v_{2},A)+3e(u_{2},B))+3e(u_{1},A)+3e(v_{1},B)

=3(2e(v_{2},A)+e(u_{1},A)+2e(u_{2},B)+e(v_{1},B)),

which implies that one of these swaps does not increase the number of class-edges in
(A,B)
.
Hence, we can assume that no vertices of
G
have their copies in both
A
and
B
.
We can now consider a balanced bipartition
(A_{G},B_{G})
of
G
, which is simply constructed by arranging vertices according to where all of their copies are in
(A,B)
.
It is easy to see that
(A_{G},B_{G})
contains at most
\frac{1}{9}\cdot n^{2}=\frac{n^{2}}{9}
class-edges.
∎

Theorem 3.3
.

If
1.4
holds for every
K_{4}
-free graph on
6n
vertices, then it also holds for every
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices.

Proof.

Let
G
be a potential counterexample to
1.4
on
n
vertices.
Let
H
be a
2
-blowup of
G
, and let
F
be a
3
-blowup of
H
.
The order of
F
is divisible by
6
, so it has a balanced partition with at most
4n^{2}
class-edges.
By
3.2
,
H
has a balanced partition with at most
{4n^{2}}/{9}
class-edges and by
3.1
,
G
has a balanced partition with at most
{n^{2}}/{9}
class-edges.
∎

3.2
Vertices of a small degree

We now show that small degree vertices are reducible.

Observation 3.4
.

If
1.4
holds for every
K_{4}
-free on
n
vertices with minimum degree at least
\frac{4n-1}{9}
, then it also holds for every
K_{4}
-free graph.

Proof.

Assume, for contradiction, that
1.4
holds for every
n
-vertex
K_{4}
-free graph with minimum degree at least
\frac{4n-1}{9}
, but not for all
K_{4}
-free graphs.
Let
G
be the smallest (with respect to the size of the vertex set)
K_{4}
-free graph
G
which does not satisfy
1.4
. Then by
3.1
its

2
-blowup also does not satisfy
1.4
.
By our assumption,
G
has at least one vertex
v
of degree at most
{(4n-2)}/{9}
, where
n
is the number of vertices in
G
.
The minimality of
G
implies that the
2
-blowup of
G-v
has a balanced bipartition
(A,B)
with at most

{(2n-2)^{2}}/{9}
class-edges.
By randomly assigning
v_{1}
to
A
or
B
, and
v_{2}
to the other part, we maintain balancedness and obtain a randomized balanced bipartition of the 2-blowup of
G
, which in expectation has at most

\frac{(2n-2)^{2}}{9}+2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2(4n-2)}{9}=\frac{4n^{2}}{9}

class-edges.
This implies that there exists a sufficiently sparse balanced bipartition of the 2-blowup of
G
, and hence of
G
, a contradiction.
∎

3.3
Tripartite graphs

1.4
has been proven in
[
3
]
to hold for tripartite graphs, and prior to this paper, this was the strongest known partial result on the conjecture.
We provide a slightly simplified proof, whose main idea is also relevant in subsequent cases.

Theorem 3.5

(Balogh, Clemen and Lidický
[
3
]
)
.

If
G
is a
K_{4}
-free tripartite graph on
n
vertices, then
\texttt{bb}(G)\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}
.

Proof.

Let
A
,
B
, and
C
be the
3
-partition of
G
with respective relative sizes (normalized by
n
) denoted as
a
,
b
, and
c
.
If any of
a
,
b
or
c
is larger than
{1}/{2}
, then the wanted sparse balanced partition exists, because by Turán’s theorem the set of
{n}/{2}
vertices outside an independent set of size
{n}/{2}
contains at most
{n^{2}}/{12}
edges.

In the other cases, we may assume that the graph is complete tripartite as adding the remaining edges does not simplify the problem.
Consider the following random process of obtaining a balanced partition:

1.

Randomly choose one of
A
,
B
or
C
.

2.

Increase the other sets to size
\frac{n}{2}
by randomly adding vertices from the chosen set.

Assume that we chose
A
in the first step.
Then, the expected number of class-edges (normalized by
n^{2}
) is exactly

\left(\frac{\frac{1}{2}-b}{a}\right)\cdot ab+\left(\frac{\frac{1}{2}-c}{a}\right)\cdot ac=\left(\frac{1}{2}-b\right)b+\left(\frac{1}{2}-c\right)c.

If we now incorporate the random choice from the first step into the formula, the expected number of class-edges changes to (keep in mind
a+b+c=1
)

\frac{1}{3}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-b\right)b+\left(\frac{1}{2}-c\right)c\right]+\frac{1}{3}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-a\right)a+\left(\frac{1}{2}-c\right)c\right]+\frac{1}{3}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-a\right)a+\left(\frac{1}{2}-b\right)b\right]

=\frac{1}{9}-\frac{2}{3}\left(a-\frac{1}{3}\right)^{2}-\frac{2}{3}\left(b-\frac{1}{3}\right)^{2}-\frac{2}{3}\left(c-\frac{1}{3}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{9}.

Hence, there exists a balanced partition with at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}
class-edges, so
{\texttt{bb}(G)\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}}
.
∎

4
Graphs with a large pair of independent sets

In this section, we prove the following relaxed version of
1.4
:

Theorem 4.1
.

If
G
is a
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices with two disjoint independent sets
{I_{1}\subseteq V(G)}
,
I_{2}\subseteq V(G)
satisfying
|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|\geq\frac{2n}{3}
, then
\texttt{bb}(G)\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}
.

Notice that
Theorem 4.1
is strictly stronger than
Theorem 3.5
, as the union of the two largest parts of tripartite partition is always greater than
{2n}/{3}
.

Theorem 4.1
is non-trivial, as a random extension of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
is insufficient even when
|I_{1}|=|I_{2}|
.

By
Theorem 3.3
, it is enough to consider only graphs of order divisible by 6.
If
G
has a pair of independent sets of total size
at least

{2n}/{3}
, it also has a pair of independent sets of total size
exactly

{2n}/{3}
.

Definition 4.2
.

A graph
G
on
n
vertices is
convenient
if it is
K_{4}
-free and contains two disjoint independent subsets
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
satisfying
|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|=\frac{2n}{3}
. W.l.o.g., we always assume that
|I_{2}|\geq|I_{1}|
, which implies that
|I_{2}|\geq\frac{n}{3}
. We write
c_{1}:=\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|,\ c_{2}:=\frac{n}{6}
and
R:=V(G)\setminus(I_{1}\cup I_{2})
.

Observation 4.3
.

If
G
is a convenient graph on
n
vertices, then there exists a balanced bipartition of
G
with at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}
class-edges, or for every edge
(u,v)\in E(R)
at least one of the following inequalities holds:

•

|N(u,v)\cap I_{1}|\leq c_{1}
,

•

|N(u,v)\cap I_{2}|\leq c_{2}.

Proof.

Assume that there is an edge
(u,v)\in E(R)
, for which both of these inequalities are false.
Let
B
be a subset of
N(u,v)\cap I_{2}
of size exactly
c_{2}
, and let
A
be a subset of
N(u,v)\cap I_{1}
of size exactly
c_{1}
.
Since
G
is
K_{4}
-free, we have
e(N(u,v))=0
, and, consequently,
e(A,B)=0
.
The set
I_{2}\cup A
is of size exactly
\frac{n}{2}
, and it has at most

e(I_{2}\setminus B,A)\leq(|I_{2}|-|B|)|A|=(|I_{2}|-c_{2})c_{1}=\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{6}\right)\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)=\frac{n^{2}}{36}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}

edges.
Additionally, by Turán’s theorem we have
e(V(G)\setminus(I_{2}\cup A))\leq{n^{2}}/{12}
.
Therefore, the total number of class-edges in the obtained balanced bipartition is at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

If
G
is a convenient graph on
n
vertices, such that
G[R]
is triangle-free, then there exists a balanced bipartition of
G
with at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}
class-edges.

Proof.

Run the following greedy algorithm starting with
A=I_{1}
,
B=I_{2}
and
R^{\prime}=R
.

1.

If there are no edges in
R^{\prime}
, randomly assign vertices remaining in
R^{\prime}
to
A
and
B
, such that
{|A|=|B|=\frac{n}{2}}
, and STOP.

2.

If
|A|=\frac{n}{2}
, then add
R^{\prime}
to
B
and STOP.

3.

If
|B|=\frac{n}{2}
, then add
R^{\prime}
to
A
and STOP.

4.

Choose an edge
(u,v)
from
R^{\prime}
.

5.

If
|N(u,v)\cap I_{1}|\leq c_{1}
, then assign
A=A\cup\{u,v\}
,
R^{\prime}=R^{\prime}\setminus\{u,v\}
, and go to Step 1.

6.

If
|N(u,v)\cap I_{2}|\leq c_{2}
, then assign
B=B\cup\{u,v\}
,
R^{\prime}=R^{\prime}\setminus\{u,v\}
, and go to Step 1.

By
4.3
, there exists a balanced bipartition of
G
with at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}
class-edges, or one of the conditions in Steps 5 and 6 always holds. Since there is a finite number of edges in
R
, the algorithm will eventually stop.

We now perform case analysis, based on which step the algorithm has exited in.

Case 1
.

Algorithm has exited in Step 2.

Assume that
(u,v)
got assigned to
A
in Step 5.
By the precondition of Step 5, we have

e(I_{1},\{u,v\})\leq|I_{1}|+c_{1}.

(1)

We obtain an identical bound for every pair of vertices that was added to
A
in Step 5.
Since the algorithm has exited in Step 2, all vertices in
A
that were not originally in
I_{1}
must have been added in Step 5.
For each of the
\frac{1}{2}(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|)
pairs added in Step 5, (
1
) holds.
Summing all of these bounds together, we obtain

e(I_{1},A\setminus I_{1})\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|\right)(|I_{1}|+c_{1}).

Using this inequality, the triangle-freeness of
G[A\setminus I_{1}]
and
G[B\setminus I_{2}]
(which follows from the triangle-freeness of
G[R]
),
and Turán’s theorem,
we provide an upper bound on the total number of class-edges in the partition
(A,B)

\displaystyle e(A)+e(B)

\displaystyle=e(I_{1},A\setminus I_{1})+e(A\setminus I_{1})+e(I_{2},B\setminus I_{2})+e(B\setminus I_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|\right)(|I_{1}|+c_{1})+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|\right)^{2}+|I_{2}|\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{6}\right)\left(\frac{7n}{12}-|I_{2}|\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{6}\right)^{2}+|I_{2}|\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-3|I_{2}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{11n|I_{2}|}{12}-\frac{n^{2}}{36}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2},

where the last inequality is equivalent to

\left(\frac{3|I_{2}|}{4}-\frac{n}{6}\right)\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)\geq 0,

which holds since
|I_{2}|\geq\frac{n}{3}
.

Case 2
.

Algorithm has exited in Step 3.

In this case, we can analogously obtain the following bound:

\displaystyle e(A)+e(B)=e(I_{1},A\setminus I_{1})+e(A\setminus I_{1})+e(I_{2},B\setminus I_{2})+e(B\setminus I_{2})

\displaystyle\leq|I_{1}|\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)(|I_{2}|+c_{2})+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{2n}{3}-|I_{2}|\right)\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{6}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{6}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)\left(|I_{2}|+\frac{n}{6}\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=-|I_{2}|^{2}+\frac{2n|I_{2}|}{3}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2},

where the last inequality is equivalent to

\frac{1}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}\geq 0.

Case 3
.

Algorithm has exited in Step 1.

Let
B^{\prime}
be the set of vertices assigned to
B
in Step 6 of the algorithm (by
A^{\prime}
denote the analogous set for Step 5).
Since there exists a perfect matching in
B^{\prime}
, and
G[R]
is triangle-free, every vertex in
R\setminus B^{\prime}
is connected to at most
{|B^{\prime}|}/{2}
vertices in
B^{\prime}
.
This observation gives us the following inequality

e(B\setminus(I_{2}\cup B^{\prime}),B^{\prime})\leq\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|-|B^{\prime}|\right).

As the algorithm exited in Step 1, there is no edge in
G[B\setminus(I_{2}\cup B^{\prime})]
.
Together with the Turán’s bound on
e(B^{\prime})
, this gives us

e(B\setminus I_{2})\leq\frac{|B^{\prime}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{2}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|-|B^{\prime}|\right)=\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{4}\left(n-2|I_{2}|-|B^{\prime}|\right).

Similarly to the previous cases, we can also prove an upper bound on
e(I_{2},B^{\prime})
:

e(I_{2},B^{\prime})\leq\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{2}(|I_{2}|+c_{2}).

Finally, we have the following bound on the number of edges between
B\setminus(B^{\prime}\cup I_{2})
and
I_{2}
:

e(I_{2},B\setminus(B^{\prime}\cup I_{2}))\leq|I_{2}|\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|-|B^{\prime}|\right).

Summing all of these bounds together, we obtain

\displaystyle e(B)

\displaystyle=e(B\setminus I_{2})+e(I_{2},B^{\prime})+e(I_{2},B\setminus(B^{\prime}\cup I_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{4}(n-2|I_{2}|-|B^{\prime}|)+\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{2}\left(|I_{2}|+c_{2}\right)+|I_{2}|\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|-|B^{\prime}|\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|B^{\prime}|^{2}}{4}-|B^{\prime}|\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)+\frac{n|I_{2}|}{2}-|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}+\frac{n|I_{2}|}{2}-|I_{2}|^{2}-\left(\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{2}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)\right)^{2},

which implies that

e(B)\leq\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}+\frac{n|I_{2}|}{2}-|I_{2}|^{2}.

(2)

We can obtain an analogous bound on the number of edges in
A
:

e(A)\leq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{4}(n-2|I_{1}|-|A^{\prime}|)+\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}(|I_{1}|+c_{1})+|I_{1}|\left(\frac{n}{2}-|I_{1}|-|A^{\prime}|\right).

This time, we substitute
|I_{1}|=\frac{2n}{3}-|I_{2}|
and
c_{1}=\frac{n}{2}-|I_{2}|
to obtain

\displaystyle e(A)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{4}\left(-\frac{n}{3}+2|I_{2}|-|A^{\prime}|\right)+\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}\left(\frac{7n}{6}-2|I_{2}|\right)+\left(\frac{2n}{3}-|I_{2}|\right)\left(-\frac{n}{6}+|I_{2}|-|A^{\prime}|\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|A^{\prime}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)-\frac{n^{2}}{9}+\frac{5n|I_{2}|}{6}-|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{9}+\frac{5n|I_{2}|}{6}-|I_{2}|^{2}-\left(\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)\right)^{2},

implying

e(A)\leq\frac{1}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{9}+\frac{5n|I_{2}|}{6}-|I_{2}|^{2}.

(3)

Combining (
2
) and (
3
), we bound the number of class-edges in the partition returned by the algorithm:

\displaystyle e(A)+e(B)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{9}+\frac{4n|I_{2}|}{3}-2|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}-2\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}+\frac{n^{2}}{9}=\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2},

which is exactly what we wanted to prove.
∎

Definition 4.5
.

For a convenient graph
G
, let
T
be the set of vertices of a maximal set of disjoint triangles in
R
and
\texttt{cl}(G)
be the graph defined as follows

•

V(\texttt{cl}(G))=V(G)
,

•

E(\texttt{cl}(G))=E(G)\setminus\{(u,v):\{u,v\}\cap T\neq\emptyset\}\cup\{(u,v):u\in T,v\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

For every convenient graph
G
and a bipartition
(A,B)
of
\texttt{cl}(G)
satisfying
I_{1}\subseteq A
,
I_{2}\subseteq B
, there exists a balanced bipartition
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
of
G
, for which

e_{G}(A^{\prime})+e_{G}(B^{\prime})\leq e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(A)+e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(B)+\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

Let
T_{1}=A\cap T
and
T_{2}=B\cap T
.
Define
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
to be the balanced partition obtained by randomly redistributing vertices from
T
to
A\setminus T_{1}
and
B\setminus T_{2}
.
In
\texttt{cl}(G)
it holds

e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(A^{\prime})+e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(B^{\prime})=e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(A)+e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(B),

as vertices from
T
are indistinguishable in
\texttt{cl}(G)
.

Notice that any edge that is present in
\texttt{cl}(G)
and is not present in
G
(or vice versa) must have at least one endpoint in
T
.
For that reason, in order to bound the difference between the number of class-edges in
G
and in
\texttt{cl}(G)
in the partition
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, it is enough to count the edges incident to
T
.

The
K_{4}
-freeness of
G
implies

e_{G}(B\setminus T,T)\leq\frac{2}{3}|B\setminus T|\cdot|T|,

as
T
is a collection of vertex disjoint triangles, so every vertex outside of it must be disconnected from at least one vertex in every one of those triangles.
Since we redistributed vertices in
T=T_{1}\cup T_{2}
randomly, we have

e_{G}(B^{\prime}\setminus T_{2},T_{2})\leq\frac{2}{3}|B^{\prime}\setminus T|\cdot|T|\cdot\frac{|T_{2}|}{|T|}=\frac{2}{3}|B^{\prime}\setminus T_{2}|\cdot|T_{2}|.

Together with a Turán bound on
e_{G}(T_{2})
, we get

\displaystyle e_{G}(B^{\prime})-e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(B^{\prime})

\displaystyle=e_{G}(T_{2})+e_{G}(B^{\prime}\setminus T_{2},T_{2})-|I_{2}||T_{2}|\leq\frac{|T_{2}|^{2}}{3}+\frac{2}{3}|B^{\prime}\setminus T_{2}||T_{2}|-|I_{2}||T_{2}|

\displaystyle=\frac{|T_{2}|^{2}}{3}+\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|T_{2}|\right)|T_{2}|-|I_{2}||T_{2}|=-\frac{|T_{2}|^{2}}{3}-\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)|T_{2}|\leq 0.

For
A^{\prime}
, we similarly derive the following bound

\displaystyle e_{G}(A^{\prime})-e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(A^{\prime})\leq-\frac{|T_{1}|^{2}}{3}-\left(|I_{1}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)|T_{1}|=-\frac{|T_{1}|^{2}}{3}+\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)|T_{1}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left(|T_{1}|+\frac{n}{3}-|T_{2}|\right)^{2}\leq\ \frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}.

Altogether, the two inequalities complete the proof of the lemma, as we get

e_{G}(A^{\prime})+e_{G}(B^{\prime})\leq e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(A)+e_{\texttt{cl}(G)}(B)+\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}.

∎

Proof of
Theorem 4.1
.

For every convenient graph
G
, by
4.4
, there exists a balanced bipartition
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
of
\texttt{cl}(G)
with at most

\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}

class-edges.
Partition
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
can be then transformed by
4.6
to a partition
(A,B)
of
G
with at most

\frac{n^{2}}{9}-\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\left(|I_{2}|-\frac{n}{3}\right)^{2}=\frac{n^{2}}{9}

class-edges.
∎

5
“Almost” complete, balanced tripartite graphs

We now present a proof for graphs that are “almost” complete, balanced tripartite.

Our proof of the general case revolves around proving that potential counterexamples to
1.4
have to be “almost” complete, balanced tripartite.
We use observations from this section to conclude that argument.

We begin with a well-known folklore lemma, and we provide a short proof for completeness.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
a,b,c
be fixed integers, such that
c\geq b\geq a
.
If
G
is a tripartite, triangle-free graph with a
3
-partition
A,B,C
, with respective sizes
a,b,c
, then
e(G)\leq ac+bc
.

Proof.

Assume
e(A,B)>0
.
Fix an arbitrary edge
(u,v)\in E(A,B)
.
Let
m(u,v)
be the number of pairs
s,t
, such that
s\in\{u,v\}
,
t\in C
and
(s,t)\notin E(G)
.
For every
w\in C
it holds that
(u,w)\notin E(G)
or
(v,w)\notin E(G)
, which implies
m(u,v)\geq c
.

This gives that
\sum_{(u,v)\in E(A,B)}m(u,v)\geq e(A,B)\cdot c
.
Since the maximum degree in
G[A\cup B]
is at most
b
, every pair
(s,t)
is counted at most
b
times.
This means that

e(G)\leq ac+bc-\frac{e(A,B)\cdot c}{b}+e(A,B)\leq ac+bc.

∎

To formalize the intuition of what an “almost” complete, balanced, tripartite graph is, we introduce the notion of
\varepsilon
-niceness.
We denote by
T(G)
the set of triangles in
G
.

Definition 5.2
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, a graph
G
on
n
vertices with
e
edges and
t
triangles is called
\varepsilon
-nice
, if it satisfies the following inequalities

\frac{2e}{n^{2}}\left(\frac{2}{3}-\frac{2e}{n^{2}}\right)<\varepsilon,

(4)

\frac{2e}{n^{2}}\left(\frac{2}{9}-\frac{6t}{n^{3}}\right)<\varepsilon,

(5)

\frac{6\sum_{\{u,v,w\}\in T(G)}n-\deg(u,v)-\deg(u,w)-\deg(v,w)}{n^{4}}<\varepsilon,

(6)

\delta(G)\geq\frac{4n-1}{9}.

(7)

Lemma 5.3
.

If
G
is an
\varepsilon
-nice counterexample to
1.4
with
\varepsilon<10^{-2}
, then
G
has disjoint independent sets
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
satisfying

{|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|+|A_{3}|\geq n(1-5\varepsilon)}

and
n(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon)<|A_{i}|<n(\frac{1}{3}+5\varepsilon)
for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

Notice that we can assume
e\geq\frac{2n^{2}}{9}
, as otherwise a random partition would have at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}
class-edges in expectation.
In addition, for
e\in\big[\frac{2n^{2}}{9},\frac{n^{2}}{4}\big]
, (
4
) cannot hold.
Because of that, we can write

\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{2}{3}-\frac{2e}{n^{2}}\right)<\varepsilon,

which is equivalent to

n^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-\varepsilon\right)<e.

(8)

Now, combining (
8
) with (
5
) gives

2\left(\frac{1}{3}-\varepsilon\right)\left(\frac{2}{9}-\frac{6t}{n^{3}}\right)<\varepsilon,

which is equivalent to

n^{3}\left(\frac{1}{27}-\frac{\varepsilon}{12(\frac{1}{3}-\varepsilon)}\right)<t,

and for
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{12}
implies that

n^{3}\left(\frac{1}{27}-\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon\right)<t.

(9)

Using inequalities (
6
) and (
9
), we conclude that there exists a triangle
u,v,w
in
G
, for which it holds (provided
\varepsilon<10^{-2}
)

n-\deg(u,v)-\deg(u,w)-\deg(v,w)<\frac{n^{4}\varepsilon}{6}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{3}(\frac{1}{27}-\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon)}<\frac{\varepsilon n}{\frac{2}{9}-2\varepsilon}<5\varepsilon n.

By
K_{4}
-freeness, the sets
N(u,v)
,
N(u,w)
, and
N(v,w)
are independent and pairwise disjoint.
As a result,
G
contains three disjoint independent sets
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
, such that
|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|+|A_{3}|>n(1-5\varepsilon)
.
Moreover, by
Theorem 4.1
, we can conclude

n\left(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon\right)<|A_{i}|<n\left(\frac{1}{3}+5\varepsilon\right)

for every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
G
be an
\varepsilon
-nice counterexample to
1.4
.
Let
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
be three vertex disjoint independent sets in
G
satisfying
|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|+|A_{3}|\geq n(1-5\varepsilon)
and

{n(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon)<|A_{i}|<n(\frac{1}{3}+5\varepsilon)}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Then, for every
B_{1}\subseteq A_{i}
,
B_{2}\subseteq A_{j}
, such that
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
,
i\neq j
,
{|B_{1}|\geq|B_{2}|\geq\sqrt{3\varepsilon}n}
, it holds
e(B_{1},B_{2})>0
.

Proof.

Denoting
S=V(G)\setminus(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})
, by Turán’s theorem in the
K_{4}
-free graph
G[S]
we have
e(S)\leq\frac{1}{3}|S|^{2}
. Therefore, if
e(B_{1},B_{2})=0
, then

\displaystyle e(G)

\displaystyle\leq|A_{1}||A_{2}|+|A_{1}||A_{3}|+|A_{2}||A_{3}|-|B_{1}||B_{2}|+|S||A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}|+\frac{1}{3}|S|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{3}|A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}|^{2}-|B_{1}||B_{2}|+|S||A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}|+\frac{1}{3}|S|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}n^{2}+\frac{1}{3}|S||A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}|-|B_{1}||B_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{3}n^{2}+\frac{5}{3}\varepsilon(1-5\varepsilon)n^{2}-3\varepsilon n^{2}\leq n^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-\varepsilon\right).

This contradicts with (
8
).
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
G
be an
\varepsilon
-nice counterexample to
1.4
with
\varepsilon<10^{-4}
.
Let
S=V(G)\setminus(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})
, where
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
are vertex disjoint independent sets satisfying
|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|+|A_{3}|\geq n(1-5\varepsilon)

and
n(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon)<|A_{i}|<n(\frac{1}{3}+5\varepsilon)
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Then for every
v\in S
there exists
i\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
e(v,A_{i})<96\varepsilon n
.

Proof.

Fix
v\in S
, and w.l.o.g. assume that
i=1
is minimizing the value
e(v,A_{i})
.
Assume for a contradiction that
sn=e(v,A_{1})\geq 96\varepsilon n
.
Degree of any vertex in
G
is at least
\frac{4n-1}{9}
.
Making a trivial assumption
n\geq 4
, we can also say that every vertex is of degree at least
\frac{15n}{36}\leq\frac{4n-1}{9}
.
As a result, both
e(v,A_{2})
and
e(v,A_{3})
must be at least
n(\frac{1}{12}-s-10\varepsilon)
.

Since the graph
N(v)\cap(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})
is tripartite and triangle-free, we use
5.1
to conclude that

e(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})\leq\frac{n^{2}}{3}-sn^{2}\left(\frac{1}{12}-s-10\varepsilon\right).

(10)

We now perform case analysis based on possible values of
s
.

Case 1
.

s<\frac{1}{12}-s-10\varepsilon
.

In this case, it is clear that quadratic function on the right-hand side of (
10
) is maximized as
s
is minimized.
Since
s\geq 96\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle e(G)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{3}-sn^{2}\left(\frac{1}{12}-s-10\varepsilon\right)+\frac{(5\varepsilon n)^{2}}{3}+5\varepsilon(1-5\varepsilon)n^{2}\leq n^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-3\varepsilon+{10200}\varepsilon^{2}\right),

which for
\varepsilon<10^{-4}
implies that
e(G)\leq n^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-\varepsilon\right)
, and this contradicts (
8
).

Case 2
.

s\geq\frac{1}{12}-s-10\varepsilon
.

In this case, we want to leverage the fact that
A_{1}
was the set minimizing the value
e(v,A_{i})
.
Because of this, both of
e(v,A_{2})
and
e(v,A_{3})
must also exceed
sn
.
We use this to improve (
10
) to

e(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})\leq\frac{n^{2}}{3}-s^{2}n^{2}.

Using
s\geq\frac{1}{24}-5\varepsilon
, we get

\displaystyle e(G)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{3}-s^{2}n^{2}+\frac{(5\varepsilon n)^{2}}{3}+5\varepsilon(1-5\varepsilon)n^{2}\leq n^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{24^{2}}+\frac{130}{24}\varepsilon-\frac{125}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\right).

Similarly to Case 1, for
\varepsilon<10^{-4}
this implies
e(G)\leq n^{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-\varepsilon\right)
, which contradicts (
8
).
∎

The previous lemmas, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, give us a deep insight into the structure of
\varepsilon
-nice counterexamples to
1.4
.
We know that
G
contains three large, disjoint, independent sets, and every vertex that does not belong to them is sparsely connected to at least one of those sets.

The most natural approach is to assign each of those vertices to the set to which they are sparsely connected, and then treat the graph as if it were tripartite.
This idea is indeed correct, and we argue that the balanced bipartition obtained similarly as in
Theorem 3.5
has sufficiently few class-edges.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
G
be an
\varepsilon
-nice counterexample to
1.4
with
{\varepsilon<10^{-4}}
.
Let
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
be disjoint independent sets in
G
satisfying
|A_{1}|+|A_{2}|+|A_{3}|\geq n(1-5\varepsilon)

and
n(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon)<|A_{i}|<n(\frac{1}{3}+5\varepsilon)
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, that exist by
5.3
.
For every
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, let
A_{i}^{\prime}
be the set containing
A_{i}
and all vertices in
V(G)\setminus(A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3})
, which has at most
96\varepsilon n
edges to
A_{i}
. By
5.5
and
\delta(G)\geq\frac{4n-1}{9}
, every vertex is in exactly one set
A_{i}^{\prime}
.
We refer to the obtained partition
A_{1}^{\prime}
,
A_{2}^{\prime}
,
A_{3}^{\prime}
as
spotty partition
.

The next lemma formalizes the intuition that adding edges inside the sets of a spotty partition is unlikely to make the problem more difficult,
as we need to remove significantly more edges between the sets in order to ensure
K_{4}
-freeness.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
G
be an
\varepsilon
-nice graph with
\varepsilon<10^{-4}
and
A_{1}^{\prime}
,
A_{2}^{\prime}
,
A_{3}^{\prime}
its spotty partition.
Let
(u,v)
be an edge contained in
A_{i}^{\prime}
for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then for the other parts
A_{j}^{\prime}
and
A_{k}^{\prime}
of the spotty partition it holds

{e(\{u,v\},A_{j}^{\prime}\cup A_{k}^{\prime})\leq 2(|A_{j}^{\prime}|+|A_{k}^{\prime}|)-n\left(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon-\sqrt{3\varepsilon}\right)}.

Proof.

Notice that
e(N(u,v)\cap A_{j}^{\prime},N(u,v)\cap A_{k}^{\prime})=0
, as otherwise there would be a
K_{4}
in
G
.
Together with
5.4
, we can infer that one of
N(u,v)\cap A_{j}^{\prime}
and
N(u,v)\cap A_{k}^{\prime}
is smaller than
\sqrt{3\varepsilon}n

(w.l.o.g. assume it is the first one).
Then,

e(\{u,v\},A_{j}^{\prime})\leq 2|A_{j}^{\prime}|-(|A_{j}^{\prime}|-\sqrt{3\varepsilon}n)\leq 2|A_{j}^{\prime}|-n\left(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon-\sqrt{3\varepsilon}\right),

which also means

e(\{u,v\},A_{j}^{\prime}\cup A_{k}^{\prime})\leq 2(|A_{j}^{\prime}|+|A_{k}^{\prime}|)-n\left(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon-\sqrt{3\varepsilon}\right),

as required.
∎

Theorem 5.8
.

1.4
holds for every
\varepsilon
-nice graph
G
with
\varepsilon<10^{-4}
.

Proof.

Let
A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}
be a spotty partition of
G
.
Let
M
be a maximal matching, whose edges are inside
A_{1}^{\prime}
,
A_{2}^{\prime}
and
A_{3}^{\prime}
(not necessarily all in the same class). Note that by the maximality of
M
, every edge inside of one of the classes
A_{1}^{\prime}
,
A_{2}^{\prime}
and
A_{3}^{\prime}
either belongs to
M
or is incident with an edge in
M
.
Denote

m(A_{i}^{\prime},A_{j}^{\prime}):=|A_{i}^{\prime}||A_{j}^{\prime}|-e(A_{i}^{\prime},A_{j}^{\prime}).

Define a partition, which we obtain by randomly distributing vertices from
A_{1}^{\prime}
to
A_{2}^{\prime}
and to
A_{3}^{\prime}
, until they reach size
\frac{n}{2}
.
Notice that this approach is sound, as each set of the spotty partition is of size at most
n(\frac{1}{3}+10\varepsilon)
, which is less than
\frac{n}{2}
.
We obtain a balanced partition, in which the expected number of class-edges is at most

\displaystyle e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})+\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|A_{2}^{\prime}|}{|A_{1}^{\prime}|}\right)e(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|A_{3}^{\prime}|}{|A_{1}^{\prime}|}\right)e(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})+\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|A_{2}^{\prime}|}{|A_{1}^{\prime}|}\right)(|A_{1}^{\prime}||A_{2}^{\prime}|-m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|A_{3}^{\prime}|}{|A_{1}^{\prime}|}\right)(|A_{1}^{\prime}||A_{3}^{\prime}|-m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})+\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{2}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{2}^{\prime}|+\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{3}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{3}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\frac{\frac{1}{6}-10\varepsilon}{\frac{1}{3}+10\varepsilon}\right)(m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})+\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{2}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{2}^{\prime}|+\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{3}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{3}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\frac{1}{2}-45\varepsilon\right)(m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})).

Now, the expected number of class-edges when the initial set is picked randomly (instead of
A_{1}^{\prime}
) is at most

\displaystyle e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})+\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{1}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{1}^{\prime}|+\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{2}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{2}^{\prime}|+\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{n}{2}-|A_{3}^{\prime}|\right)|A_{3}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{2}{3}\left(\frac{1}{2}-45\varepsilon\right)(m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})+m(A_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}+e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})-\left(\frac{1}{3}-30\varepsilon\right)(m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})+m(A_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})).

We can now see that this partition is sparse enough, as long as

e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})\leq\left(\frac{1}{3}-30\varepsilon\right)(m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})+m(A_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})).

(11)

Notice that by
5.5
the maximum degree in
G[A_{i}^{\prime}]
is bounded by
96\varepsilon n+5\varepsilon n
, which means

e(A_{1}^{\prime})+e(A_{2}^{\prime})+e(A_{3}^{\prime})\leq 202\varepsilon|M|n.

We now apply
5.7
to every edge in
M
.
Since
M
is a matching, every missing edge between a pair of
A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime}
and
A_{3}^{\prime}
is counted at most twice.
This gives a lower bound on the total number of missing edges

\frac{1}{2}|M|n\left(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)\leq m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime})+m(A_{1}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime})+m(A_{2}^{\prime},A_{3}^{\prime}).

We can now see that (
11
) is true, whenever

202\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{3}-5\varepsilon-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)\left(\frac{1}{3}-30\varepsilon\right),

which holds for
\varepsilon<10^{-4}
.
∎

6
Flag algebras

To complete the proof for the general case, we employ the powerful framework of flag algebras developed by Razborov in
[
24
]
.

Flag algebras are used to obtain bounds on asymptotic densities of induced substructures in combinatorial objects.
As of now, the most frequent applications were in graphs (
[
15
,
16
,
23
,
25
]
), but flags also proved to be useful in other use cases, such as oriented graphs (
[
17
,
27
]
), hypergraphs (
[
1
,
13
]
), hypercubes (
[
4
]
) or permutations (
[
5
]
). The method was also successfully applied to problems that are not expressed in terms of subgraph densities, but can be translated to such a setting by introducing colors on vertices or edges in a particular way (
[
14
,
20
]
).

The process of obtaining bounds using the flag algebra method can be partially automated with the help of semidefinite programming.
A proper introduction to this framework is beyond the scope of this paper.
For this purpose, we refer the interested readers to the original paper by Razborov
[
24
]
or surveys
[
18
,
26
,
30
]
.

For the rest of this section we focus on defining the notation that we use for flags, which is very similar to the one used in
[
3
]
.

We only operate on the flags from the theory of simple, undirected,
2
-vertex-colored
K_{4}
-free graphs.
We color the vertices with blue and red.

We denote the flags from the algebra of the unlabeled flags by directly depicting the graph using round vertices.
For instance, this is the flag for the unlabeled triangle with two blue vertices and a single red vertex:

}}.

and signify the injective mapping

\operatorname{im}(\theta)
are also square, rather than round.

}}.

We also use the white color as a wildcard for any color.
More formally, a white vertex is syntactic sugar for the sum of the flags which are isomorphic to the template up to the color of this vertex.
When only one vertex in the template is white, this is equivalent to a sum of two flags, where we replace the white vertex with a blue one and a red one.
For example

7
General case

In this section we leverage prior observations and the flag algebraic framework to conclude the proof in the general case.

Definition 7.1
.

For a
K_{4}
-free graph
G
, we define its
degree coloring
by coloring each vertex
v\in V(G)
red if
\deg(v)\geq{|V(G)|}/{2}
, and blue if
\deg(v)<{|V(G)|}/{2}
.

Theorem 7.2
.

If
G
is a
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices, then
{\texttt{bb}(G)\leq\frac{n^{2}}{9}}
.

Proof.

Let
G
be the smallest (with respect to the number of vertices) counterexample with the degree coloring.
By
3.4
we can assume that
G
has no vertices of degree less than
({4n-1})/{9}
.

Let
W
be the
2
-vertex-colored graphon obtained by considering the infinite sequence of increasing blowups of
G
.
For the rest of the proof, we define flag inequalities that
W
would have to satisfy and use semidefinite programming to prove that
W
cannot exist.

To obtain the first inequality, assume that there is at least one blue vertex in
G
, say
v
. By the definition of the coloring, the degree of
v
is less than
{n}/{2}
.
Consider a balanced bipartition, where one part is a random extension of
N(v)
to size
{n}/{2}
and the second one is its complement.
Since
G
is a counterexample to
1.4
, we know that there are at least
{n^{2}}/{9}
class-edges inside this bipartition.
This means that if we choose
v
at random, then the expected number of edges also cannot be below
{n^{2}}/{9}
.
We can write this as the following inequality for
G

\frac{n^{2}}{9}\leq e(N(v))+e(N(v),V(G)\setminus N(v))\cdot\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|N(v)|}{n-|N(v)|}+e(V(G)\setminus N(v))\cdot\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}}{n-|N(v)|}\right)^{2}.

(12)

Notice that this inequality heavily relies on the assumption that
v
is blue.
It is clear that such inequality must hold for any
k
-blowup of
G
, which we can use to define an analogous relationship for
W
.
To do this, we rely on the following correspondence between limits of densities in
k
-blowups of
G
and flags of type

1

:

•

(13)

It is easy to see that if (
13
) did not hold for
W
, then there would exist some
k
(possibly very large), such that (
12
) would not hold for the
k
-blowup of
G
.
Notice that (
13
) is still well-defined and holds even when there are no blue vertices in
G
(e.g., in a complete balanced tripartite graph).

We now define similar partitions, but based on more non-trivial roots (edge and cherry).
Assume that there exists at least one edge in
G
whose both endpoints are red, and fix one of them
(u,v)
.
We now partition
V(G)
into following parts based on
(u,v)
:

L:=N(u)\setminus N(v),\quad\quad\quad\quad R:=N(u,v),\quad\quad\quad\quad C:=V(G)\setminus(L\cup R).

The next balanced bipartition is a random extension of
L
and
R
to size
\frac{n}{2}
with vertices from
C
.
Since
L\subseteq V(G)\setminus N(v)
, the maximum size of
L
is bounded by
\frac{n}{2}
, because
\deg(v)\geq{n}/{2}
.
In addition, the size of
R
is upper-bounded by
{n}/{2}
, because otherwise we would have an independent set of size
{n}/{2}
, which would give a balanced bipartition with at most
{n^{2}}/12
class-edges by applying Turán’s theorem to its complement.
Therefore, this bipartition is well-defined.
A lower bound on the expected number of class-edges inside this partition gives the following inequality

\frac{n^{2}}{9}\leq e(L)+e(R)+\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|L|}{|C|}e(L,C)+\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|R|}{|C|}e(R,C)+\left[\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|L|}{|C|}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\frac{n}{2}-|R|}{|C|}\right)^{2}\right]\cdot e(C).

Figure 1:
Different kinds of class-edges in the random bipartition based on the edge
(u,v)
.

This time, limits of densities relevant to this partition are in the flag algebra of type

1
2

:

}}\right)^{2}\right]\right\rrbracket.

For the next partition, we redefine sets
L
,
R
and
C
as:

•

L:=N(u)\setminus N(v)\cup(V(G)\setminus(N(u)\cup N(v)))
,

•

R:=N(v)\setminus N(u)
,

•

C:=V(G)\setminus(L\cup R)=N(u,v)
.

The obtained partition is well-defined, because
L\subseteq V(G)\setminus N(v)
,
R\subseteq V(G)\setminus N(u)
and both vertices are red. After transformations analogous to the previous partition, this gives

Notice that there are no edges in
C
, which explains the absence of the final summand and lowers the power of the common denominator.

The final partition we use is based on a cherry.
We fix a red cherry
(u,v,w)
(where
v
is connected to both
u
and
w
) and define sets
L
,
R
and
C
as

•

L:=(N(u)\cup N(w))\setminus N(v)
,

•

R:=N(v)\setminus N(u)\cup(V(G)\setminus(N(u)\cup N(v)\cup N(w)))
,

•

C:=V(G)\setminus(L\cup R)=N(u,v)
.

Since
L\subseteq V(G)\setminus N(v)
and
R\subseteq V(G)\setminus N(u)
, it holds that
|L|,|R|\leq\frac{n}{2}
.
After transformations analogous to the previous partition, i.e., randomly extending sets
A
and
B
with vertices from
C
until the size of
\frac{n}{2}
, this partition gives

Similarly, there are no edges within
C
, which lowers the power of the common denominator and reduces the total number of vertices in the final linear combination of flags.
This is crucial for the tractability of the SDP instance that is used to finish this proof.

}}\right\rrbracket<10^{-9}.

(17)

Exact calculations can be viewed at
https://github.com/ignacy123/k4-free-balanced-bipartitions
.

By
Theorem 5.8
we know that
G
cannot be
\varepsilon
-nice with
\varepsilon=10^{-5}
.
It is also easy to see that no
k
-blowup of
G
can be
\varepsilon
-nice, for any
k\geq 1
.
However, (
17
) implies that sufficiently large
k
-blowups of
G
are
10^{-9}
-nice, which is a contradiction.
This shows that
W
cannot exist and, in turn, neither can
G
.
It follows that the balanced bipartite distance of every
K_{4}
-free graph on
n
vertices is at most
\frac{n^{2}}{9}
.
∎

8
Acknowledgements

The computer-assisted part of this work is based on the flag program originally developed by Bernard Lidický (which was used in
[
6
]
).
The authors are thankful for his permission for modifying and publishing the modified version of the program.

The authors would like to thank American Institute of Mathematics (AIM) for hosting the workshop “Flag algebras and extremal combinatorics”, where three of the authors worked on this project.

The authors thank Bernard Lidický and Felix Clemen for fruitful discussions.
